A Note on Multi-Oriented Graph Complexes and Deformation Quantization of Lie Bialgebroids
Kevin MORAND ab

Abstract
Universal solutions to deformation quantization problems can be conveniently classified by the cohomology of suitable graph complexes. In particular, the deformation quantizations of (finite-dimensional) Poisson manifolds and Lie bialgebras are characterised by an action of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group via one-colored directed and oriented graphs, respectively. In this note, we study the action of multi-oriented graph complexes on Lie bialgebroids and their “quasi” generalisations. Using results due to T. Willwacher and M. Živković on the cohomology of (multi)-oriented graphs, we show that the action of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group on Lie bialgebras and quasi-Lie bialgebras can be generalised to quasi-Lie bialgebroids via graphs with two colors, one of them being oriented. However, this action generically fails to preserve the subspace of Lie bialgebroids. By resorting to graphs with two oriented colors, we instead show the existence of an obstruction to the quantization of a generic Lie bialgebroid in the guise of a new 𝖫𝗂𝖾 ∞ \mathsf{Lie}_{\infty} -algebra structure non-trivially deforming the “big bracket” for Lie bialgebroids. This exotic 𝖫𝗂𝖾 ∞ \mathsf{Lie}_{\infty} -structure can be interpreted as the equivalent in d = 3 d=3 of the Kontsevich–Shoikhet obstruction to the quantization of infinite-dimensional Poisson manifolds (in d = 2 d=2 ). We discuss the implications of these results with respect to a conjecture due to P. Xu regarding the existence of a quantization map for Lie bialgebroids.



keywords
deformation quantization; Kontsevich’s graphs; Lie bialgebroids; Grothendieck–Teichmüller group

Introduction

Graph complexes play an essential rôle in the understanding of the deformation quantization of various algebraic and geometric structures, the paradigmatic example thereof being the Kontsevich graph complex and its relation to the deformation quantization problem for (finite-dimensional) Poisson manifolds
[
32
]
. In particular, the space of Kontsevich quantization maps is acted upon by the pro-unipotent group exponentiating the zeroth cohomology of the Kontsevich graph complex of directed graphs
[
12
]
. As shown by T. Willwacher
[
71
]
, the latter is isomorphic to the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group
\mathsf{GRT}_{1}
-- introduced by V. Drinfeld
1
1

1

Based on a suggestion due to A. Grothendieck in his
Esquisse d’un Programme

[
27
]
who proposed to study the combinatorial properties of the absolute Galois group
\text{Gal}\big(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}\big)
via its natural action on the tower of Teichmüller groupoids.

[
17
]
in the context of the absolute Galois group
\text{Gal}\big(\bar{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}\big)
and the theory of quasi-Hopf algebras -- so that the space of Kontsevich maps
2
2

2

More precisely, the space of homotopy classes
[
11
]
of stable
[
12
]
formality morphisms is a
\mathsf{GRT}_{1}
-torsor.
is a
\mathsf{GRT}_{1}
-torsor
[
33
]
. Since its inception, the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group appeared in a variety of mathematical contexts such as the Kashiwara–Vergne conjecture, multiple zeta values, rational homotopy of the
\mathbf{E}_{2}
-operad, etc.

In the present context of graph complexes, another incarnation of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group can be found in relation to the deformation quantization problem for Lie bialgebras via the action of the graph complex of
oriented
graphs
[
72
]
in dimension
d=3
.
3
3

3

Recall that the parameter
d
corresponds to the dimension of the source manifold of the relevant AKSZ
\sigma
-model
[
1
]
. The latter is related to the degree
n
of the corresponding target manifold via
d=n+1
and is therefore independent of the dimension of the associated algebro-geometric structure (Poisson manifold, Lie bialgebra, etc.). Consistently, it relates to the dimension of the compactified configuration spaces of points of the associated de Rham field theories
[
45
,
46
]
. Therefore, any graph complex related to Poisson manifolds has dimension
d=2
while the ones related to Lie bialgebras and generalisations thereof have dimension
d=3
.
The latter action generalises to Lie-quasi bialgebras
4
4

4

As well as their dual, referred to as quasi-Lie bialgebras in the following, see footnote
14
for terminology.
and furthermore provides a rationale for the classifying rôle played by the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group on the space of quantization maps for Lie and Lie-quasi bialgebras à la Etingof–Kazhdan
[
20
,
61
]
. The oriented graph complex also plays a crucial rôle regarding the obstruction theory to the existence of a universal quantization of infinite-dimensional Poisson manifolds
[
72
]
. The corresponding obstruction lives in the first order cohomology of the oriented graph complex in
d=2
which is a one-dimensional space spanned by the so-called Kontsevich–Shoikhet cocycle. When represented on the space of (infinite-dimensional) polyvector fields, the latter yields an exotic
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-structure
[
65
]
deforming non-trivially the Schouten bracket. Further, the zeroth order cohomology of the oriented graph complex (in
d=2
) vanishes thus preventing the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group to play a classifying rôle for quantizations of infinite-dimensional Poisson manifolds. The deformation quantization problem for infinite-dimensional Poisson manifolds thus differs essentially from the finite-dimensional case and this discrepancy can be traced back to the fact that their respective deformation theory is acted upon by a different graph complex (directed vs. oriented). Deformation quantization problems can then be partitioned into different classes according to the cohomology of the graph complex acting on them. We distinguish between three main classes:

•

A
no-go
class comprising the deformation quantization problems for infinite-dimensional Poisson manifolds:

1.

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group plays no classifying rôle regarding the universal deformations
(and hence quantizations).

2.

There exists a potential obstruction to the existence of universal quantizations.

•

A
yes-go
class comprising the deformation quantization problems for (finite-dimensional) Poisson manifolds and Lie-(quasi) bialgebras for which:

1.

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group plays a classifying rôle.

2.

There is (conjecturally) no generic obstruction to the existence of universal quantizations.

•

A
middle way
class comprising the deformation quantization problems for Courant algebroids:

1.

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group plays no classifying rôle; rather deformations are generated by the triangle cocycle as well as by conformal rescalings associated with trivalent graphs.

2.

There is no generic obstruction to the existence of universal quantizations.

In the present note, we add two threads to this on-going story by introducing some new universal models for the deformation theory of Lie bialgebroids and their “quasi” versions. Lie bialgebroids have been introduced by Mackenzie–Xu
[
42
]
as linearisations of Poisson groupoids and constitute a common generalisation of the notions of Poisson manifolds and Lie bialgebras. The corresponding quantization problem unifies the quantization problems for (finite-dimensional) Poisson manifolds and Lie bialgebras. It was spelled out by P. Xu
[
74
,
76
]
who then conjectured that any Lie bialgebroid is quantizable. The main result of this note consists in providing some arguments for the non-existence of
universal
quantizations of Lie bialgebroids. This is done by exhibiting a potential obstruction to the existence of a universal quantization map for Lie bialgebroids in the guise of an exotic
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-structure on the deformation complex of Lie bialgebroids. The latter is a non-trivial deformation of the so-called “big bracket” for Lie bialgebroids and can be considered as an avatar in
d=3
of the Kontsevich–Shoikhet obstruction to the quantization of infinite-dimensional Poisson manifolds. Our main result is stated as follows:

Theorem 1.1

(no-go)
.

The deformation complex of Lie bialgebroids is endowed with an exotic
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-structure deforming non-trivially the big bracket of Lie bialgebroids.

It follows from the above considerations that the deformation quantization problem for Lie bialgebroids differs essentially from its Lie bialgebra counterpart and is in fact more akin to the one for infinite-dimensional Poisson manifolds, i.e., it belongs to the no-go class.
The origin of this obstruction can be traced back to an action of the graph complex of
bi-oriented
graphs (i.e., graphs with two oriented colors) on the deformation theory of Lie bialgebroids. Relaxing the orientation of one of the colors yields an action on the deformation theory of Lie-quasi bialgebroids (and their dual). As a corollary, we find an action of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group on Lie-quasi bialgebroids generalising the one on Lie-(quasi) bialgebras.

Theorem 1.2

(yes-go)
.

The Grothendieck–Teichmüller group acts via
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-automorphisms on the deformation complex of both Lie-quasi bialgebroids and quasi-Lie bialgebroids.

Hence, the deformation quantization problem for Lie-quasi bialgebroids differs from its Lie bialgebroid counterpart and resembles more closely the one for Lie bialgebras, i.e., it belongs to the Yes-go class. We conjecture on the basis of these results the existence – given a Drinfeld associator – of a universal quantization for Lie-quasi bialgebroids (and their dual).

Organisation of this paper.
The original universal model introduced by M. Kontsevich
[
32
]
takes advantage of the graded geometric interpretation of Poisson manifolds as
7
7

7

In the remaining of the text, we use the prefix dg to refer to differential graded objects.
dg symplectic manifolds of degree 1. Correspondingly, Section
2
reviews the formulation of Lie bialgebras and Lie bialgebroids (as well as their generalisations Lie-quasi, quasi-Lie and proto-Lie) as particular dg symplectic manifolds of degree 2. This graded geometric description of the deformation theory of Lie bialgebroids and generalisations thereof will be instrumental in formulating associated universal models in Section
4
.

The class of universal models introduced in this note involves
multi-oriented
graphs, as introduced in
[
77
]
and studied in
[
47
]
in the context of multi-oriented props and their representations on homotopy algebras with branes. The main definitions and results regarding the cohomology of multi-oriented graph complexes are reviewed in Section
3
.

Following these two review sections, we introduce our main results in Section
4
. We start by reviewing the known action of the (one-colored) oriented graph complex on Lie-(quasi) bialgebras in Section
4.2
and then move on to the Lie bialgebroid case in Section
4.3
. Using the cohomological results reviewed in Section
3
, we prove our main results regarding the existence of an exotic
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-structure for Lie bialgebroids (Theorem
1.1
) and the action of the Grothendieck–Teichmüller group on Lie-quasi bialgebroids (Theorem
1.2
).

In view of the results of Section
4.3
, we formulate two conjectures in Section
4.4
: a no-go (Conjecture
4.11
) regarding the existence of (universal) quantizations for Lie bialgebroids and a yes-go (Conjecture
4.12
) regarding the one of Lie-quasi bialgebroids (and their dual).

Two appendices conclude the present note. Appendix
A
reviews the (ungraded) geometric formulation of Lie bialgebroids and related notions, to be compared with the graded geometric interpretation of Section
2
. Appendix
B
contains explicit formulae and additional results regarding the graph cocycle generating the exotic
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-structure for Lie bialgebroids.

Conventions.
Throughout the text, we work over a ground field
{\mathbb{K}}
of characteristic zero. The operads introduced in the text live in the category of (graded) vector spaces over
{\mathbb{K}}
. Given a graded
{\mathbb{K}}
-vector space
\mathfrak{g}:=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}^{k}
, the
n
-suspended graded vector space
\mathfrak{g}[n]
is defined via its homogeneous components
\mathfrak{g}[n]^{k}:=\mathfrak{g}^{k+n}
. We will denote
s\colon\mathfrak{g}[n]\to\mathfrak{g}
the corresponding suspension map of intrinsic degree
n
.

2
Graded geometry

It is a well-known result that a Lie algebra structure on a vector space
\mathfrak{g}
yields a differential structure on the exterior algebra
\wedge^{\bullet}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
in the guise of the Chevalley–Eilenberg differential. The exterior algebra can equivalently be recast as the algebra of functions on the shifted vector space
\mathfrak{g}[1]
seen as a graded manifold of degree 1 on which the Chevalley–Eilenberg differential defines a homological vector field. Such a supergeometric formulation of Lie algebras was generalised by A.Yu. Vaintrob
[
67
]
who showed a bijective correspondence between dg manifolds (or
{\rm\mathsf{NQ}}
-manifolds) of degree 1 and Lie algebroids. On the other hand, it was shown by D. Roytenberg
[
58
,
59
]
that dg symplectic manifolds (or
{\rm\mathsf{NPQ}}
-manifolds) of degree 1 (resp. of degree 2) are in bijective correspondence with Poisson manifolds (resp. Courant algebroids). Our aim in this section is to review how Lie bialgebra and Lie bialgebroid structures (and generalisations
8
8

8

As reviewed in Appendix
A
.
) can be naturally recast as Hamiltonian functions for a specific graded Poisson algebra of functions on a graded manifold (as pioneered in
[
39
]
, cf.
[
37
,
58
]
for details and related constructions). We start by reviewing this graded geometric construction for Lie bialgebras (including proto-Lie, Lie-quasi and quasi-Lie bialgebras) in Section
2.1
and then move on to the Lie bialgebroid counterparts of these notions in Section
2.2
.

2.1
Lie bialgebras

Lie bialgebra structures (and generalisations thereof) on a vector space
\mathfrak{g}
can be conveniently encoded into particular Hamiltonian functions on the graded manifold
T^{*}(\mathfrak{g}[1])\simeq(\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1]
with homogeneous coordinates
9
9

9

The subscript denotes the corresponding degree.

\big\{\underset{1}{\xi^{a}},\underset{1}{\zeta_{a}}\big\}
, with
a\in\left\{1,\dots,\dim\mathfrak{g}\right\}
. The latter is a graded symplectic manifold with symplectic 2-form
\Omega={\rm d}\xi^{a}\wedge{\rm d}\zeta_{a}
of degree
2
. The associated Poisson bracket of degree
-2
acts on homogeneous functions in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
as

\displaystyle\big\{{f},{g}\big\}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\Omega}=(-1)^{|f|}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial\xi^{a}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial\zeta_{a}}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial\zeta_{a}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial\xi^{a}}\right).

(2.1)

The graded Poisson bracket (
2.1
) can be seen as the graded geometric formulation of the “big bracket” (introduced by Y. Kosmann–Schwarzbach
[
35
]
) acting on
\wedge^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})\simeq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
.

Upgrading the graded symplectic manifold
(\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1]
to a dg symplectic manifold (or
{\rm\mathsf{NPQ}}
-manifold)
10
10

10

Or equivalently, endowing the graded Poisson algebra of functions
\big(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])},\cdot,\left\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\right\}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\Omega}\big)
with a compatible differential.
allows to define various algebraic structures. A differential structure on a graded symplectic manifold is given by a vector field
{\mathsf{Q}}
of degree 1 being homological (i.e.,
\left[{\mathsf{Q}},{\mathsf{Q}}\right]_{\mathsf{Lie}}=0
) with respect to the graded Lie bracket of vector fields and compatible with the symplectic 2-form (i.e.,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{Q}}\Omega=0
). This last compatibility relation ensures
11
11

11

Via Cartan’s homotopy formula, cf.
[
59
, Lemma 2.2]
.
that
{\mathsf{Q}}
is necessarily a Hamiltonian vector field, i.e., there exists a function of degree 3 called the Hamiltonian satisfying the structure equation
\big\{{{\mathscr{H}}},{{\mathscr{H}}}\big\}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\Omega}=0
and such that
{\mathsf{Q}}:=\big\{{{\mathscr{H}}},{\cdot}\big\}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\Omega}
. The most general function of degree 3 on
(\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1]
reads explicitly as
12
12

12

The signs and coefficients are chosen for later convenience.

\displaystyle{\mathscr{H}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}f_{ab}{}^{c}\xi^{a}\xi^{b}\zeta_{c}-\tfrac{1}{2}C_{c}{}^{ab}\zeta_{a}\zeta_{b}\xi^{c}+\tfrac{1}{6}\varphi^{abc}\zeta_{a}\zeta_{b}\zeta_{c}+\tfrac{1}{6}\psi_{abc}\xi^{a}\xi^{b}\xi^{c},

(2.2)

where
13
13

13

Here and in the following, round (resp. square) brackets of indices will denote (skew)symmetrisation.

f_{ab}{}^{c}=f_{[ab]}{}^{c}
,
C_{c}{}^{ab}=C_{c}{}^{[ab]}
,
\varphi^{abc}=\varphi^{[abc]}
and
\psi_{abc}=\psi_{[abc]}
.

The Hamiltonian condition
\big\{{{\mathscr{H}}},{{\mathscr{H}}}\big\}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\Omega}=0
translates as a set of 5 constraints on the defining maps
\left\{f,C,\varphi,\psi\right\}
:

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{D}_{1}{}_{abc}{}^{d}:=-f_{e[a}{}^{d}f_{bc]}{}^{e}-\psi_{e[ab}C_{c]}{}^{ed}=0,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{D}_{2}{}_{d}{}^{abc}:=-C_{d}{}^{e[a}C_{e}{}^{bc]}-\varphi^{e[ab}f_{ed}{}^{c]}{}=0,

(2.4)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{D}_{3}{}_{ab}{}^{cd}:=2f_{e[a}{}^{[c}C_{b]}{}^{d]e}-\tfrac{1}{2}f_{ab}{}^{e}C_{e}{}^{cd}-\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{eab}\varphi^{ecd}=0,

(2.5)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{D}_{4}{}^{abcd}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi^{e[ab}C_{e}{}^{cd]}=0,

(2.6)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{D}_{5}{}_{abcd}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{e[ab}f_{cd]}{}^{e}=0.

(2.7)

A set of maps
\left\{f,C,\varphi,\psi\right\}
satisfying the constraints (
2.3
)–(
2.7
) form the components of a
proto-Lie bialgebra
on
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{*})
(cf. Appendix
A
for a definition) whose deformation theory is therefore controlled by the dg Lie algebra
14
14

14

Note that the graded Poisson bracket has intrinsic degree
-2
on
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
. To recover the usual grading, one needs to consider the 2-suspension
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}[2]
.

\big(\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])},{\mathsf{Q}},\left\{{\cdot},{\cdot}\right\}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\Omega}\big)
. Proto-Lie bialgebras thus constitute the most general notion in the bialgebra realm and other structures (Lie-quasi, quasi-Lie and Lie bialgebras) will be defined as particular cases thereof.

The remainder of this section will therefore introduce several particular graded Poisson subalgebras of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
whose Hamiltonian functions will encode various sub-classes of proto-Lie bialgebras. Let us start by defining the subspace
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
as
\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}:=\left\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}\,\big|\,f|_{\zeta=0}=0\right\}
. In plain words, the subspace
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}
is obtained by discarding all functions of the form
\psi_{a_{1}\cdots a_{m}}\xi^{a_{1}}\cdots\xi^{a_{m}}
, for arbitrary values of
m\geq 0
. It can be easily checked that
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}
is preserved by both the pointwise product of functions and the graded Poisson bracket (
2.1
) and thus defines a graded Poisson subalgebra of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
. The most general Hamiltonian function of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}
reads as (
2.2
) with
\psi\equiv 0
, where the maps
\left\{f,C,\varphi\right\}
satisfy (
2.3
)–(
2.6
) with
\psi\equiv 0
. In particular, imposing
\psi\equiv 0
in equation (
2.3
) ensures that the map
f
defines a genuine Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}
(while the structure defined on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is still “quasi” due to the presence of
\varphi
). The resulting equations reproduce the defining conditions of a
Lie-quasi bialgebra
on
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{*})
as introduced by Drinfeld in
[
16
]
as semi-classicalisation of the notion of quasi-bialgebra.
15
15

15

Remark that Lie-quasi bialgebras were denoted “quasi-Lie bialgebras” in
[
16
]
. We follow the terminology used in
[
37
]
where the term Lie-quasi bialgebras is used for Lie algebras which fail to be Lie bialgebras (so that they are only “quasi” bialgebras) while the term quasi-Lie was reserved for the dual counterpart (not considered in
[
16
]
) where the Jacobi identity for
f
is only “quasi” satisfied.

Dually to the previous case, one defines the graded Poisson subalgebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm quasi-Lie}}
as the subspace obtained by discarding all functions of the form
\varphi^{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\zeta_{a_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{a_{n}}
, for all
n\geq 0
, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm quasi-Lie}}:=\left\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}\,\big|\,f|_{\xi=0}=0\right\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
. The most general Hamiltonian function of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\rm quasi-Lie}
reads as (
2.2
) with
\varphi\equiv 0
, where the functions
\left\{f,C,\psi\right\}
satisfy (
2.3
)–(
2.5
) and (
2.7
) with
\varphi\equiv 0
. Dually to the Lie-quasi case, setting
\varphi\equiv 0
in equation (
2.4
) ensures that the map
C
defines a genuine Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(while the structure defined on
\mathfrak{g}
is only “quasi” Lie due to the presence of
\psi
). The resulting equations reproduce the defining conditions of a
quasi-Lie bialgebra
on
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{*})
as introduced and studied in
[
3
,
35
]
.

Finally, let us define the subspace
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\rm Lie}:=\left\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}\,\big|\,f|_{\xi=0}=0\text{ and }f|_{\zeta=0}=0\right\}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\rm Lie}
is defined as the intersection
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\rm Lie}:=\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}\cap\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm quasi-Lie}}
between the two previous Poisson subalgebras. The latter subspace can again be checked to be a graded Poisson subalgebra of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
obtained by discarding all functions of the form
\psi_{a_{1}\cdots a_{m}}\xi^{a_{1}}\cdots\xi^{a_{m}}
and
\varphi^{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}\zeta_{a_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{a_{n}}
for all
m,n\geq 0
. In particular, the most general Hamiltonian function of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\rm Lie}
reads as (
2.2
) with
\varphi\equiv 0
and
\psi\equiv 0
, where the functions
\left\{f,C\right\}
satisfy (
2.3
)–(
2.5
) with
\varphi\equiv 0
and
\psi\equiv 0
. In particular, constraint (
2.3
)
(
resp. (
2.4
)
)
ensures that the map
f
(resp.
C
) defines a genuine Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}
(resp.
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
). These two Lie algebras are furthermore compatible with each other due to (
2.5
) and hence define a
Lie bialgebra
on
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{*})
(cf. (
A.1
)). We sum up the previous discussion in the following proposition:

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a vector space. The following correspondences hold:

•

Hamiltonians in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
are in bijective correspondence with proto-Lie bialgebra structures on
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{*})
.

•

“
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}
“      Lie-quasi bialgebra   “  .

•

“
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm quasi-Lie}}
“      quasi-Lie bialgebra   “  .

•

“
\mathcal{A}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\text{\rm Lie}}
“      Lie bialgebra    “  .

This interpretation of the deformation theory for Lie bialgebras and generalisations as graded Poisson algebras will be put to use in Section
4
where will be discussed universal models thereof.
In the next section, we turn to the generalisation of this graded geometric interpretation to the larger class of Lie bialgebroids and variations thereof.

2.2
Lie bialgebroids

Letting
E\overset{\pi}{\to}\mathscr{M}
be a vector bundle over the smooth (finite-dimensional) manifold
16
16

16

The restriction from the “algebroid” case to the “algebra” case can be done by assuming that
\mathscr{M}
is the one-point manifold so that
E\simeq\mathfrak{g}
becomes a
{\mathbb{K}}
-vector space.
\mathscr{M}
, the relevant graded Poisson algebra is the algebra of functions of the graded symplectic manifold
T^{*}[2]E[1]
, defined as the (2-shifted) cotangent bundle of the (1-shifted
17
17

17

Here and in the following,
A[n]
will denote the vector bundle obtained by shifting the grading of the fiber of the vector bundle
A
by
n
.
) vector bundle
E
, and denoted
{\mathscr{E}}\equiv T^{*}[2]E[1]
in the following.
The graded manifold
{\mathscr{E}}
is of degree 2 and can be locally
18
18

18

Correspondingly, all formulae appearing in this note will be local.
coordinatised by the set of homogeneous coordinates
\big\{\underset{0}{x^{\mu}},\underset{1}{\xi^{a}},\underset{1}{\zeta_{a}},\underset{2}{p_{\mu}}\big\}
so that the symplectic 2-form of degree
2
can be written as

\Omega={\rm d}x^{\mu}\wedge{\rm d}p_{\mu}+{\rm d}\xi^{a}\wedge{\rm d}\zeta_{a}.

The associated Poisson bracket of degree
-2
acts as follows on homogeneous functions
f,g\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{({\mathscr{E}})}
:

\displaystyle\big\{{f},{g}\big\}^{E}_{\Omega}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{\mu}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial p_{\mu}}-\frac{\partial f}{\partial p_{\mu}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial x^{\mu}}+(-1)^{|f|}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial\xi^{a}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial\zeta_{a}}+\frac{\partial f}{\partial\zeta_{a}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial\xi^{a}}\right).

(2.8)

The latter is sometimes referred to as the “big bracket” for Lie bialgebroids. Upgrading the graded symplectic manifold
{\mathscr{E}}
to a dg symplectic manifold will allow to define various geometric structures. Following the same path as in the bialgebra case, we introduce a compatible differential through a Hamiltonian function. The most general function of degree 3 on
{\mathscr{E}}
reads

\displaystyle{\mathscr{H}}=\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}(x)\xi^{a}p_{\mu}-\tfrac{1}{2}f_{ab}{}^{c}(x)\xi^{a}\xi^{b}\zeta_{c}+R^{a|\mu}(x)\zeta_{a}p_{\mu}-\tfrac{1}{2}C_{c}{}^{ab}(x)\zeta_{a}\zeta_{b}\xi^{c}

\displaystyle\hphantom{{\mathscr{H}}=}{}+\tfrac{1}{6}\varphi^{abc}(x)\zeta_{a}\zeta_{b}\zeta_{c}+\tfrac{1}{6}\psi_{abc}(x)\xi^{a}\xi^{b}\xi^{c},

(2.9)

where
\left\{\rho,f,R,C,\varphi,\psi\right\}
are functions on the base space
\mathscr{M}
, with symmetries
f_{ab}{}^{c}=f_{[ab]}{}^{c}
,
C_{c}{}^{ab}=C_{c}{}^{[ab]}
,
\varphi^{abc}=\varphi^{[abc]}
and
\psi_{abc}=\psi_{[abc]}
. Imposing the Hamiltonian constraint
\big\{{{\mathscr{H}}},{{\mathscr{H}}}\big\}^{E}_{\Omega}=0
yields a set of 9 conditions on the defining functions
\left\{\rho,f,R,C,\varphi,\psi\right\}
that we denote as follows

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{C}_{1}{}_{ab}{}^{\mu}:=2\rho_{[a}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\rho_{b]}{}^{\mu}-\rho_{c}{}^{\mu}f_{ab}{}^{c}+R^{c|\mu}\psi_{cab}=0,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{C}_{2}{}_{abc}{}^{d}:=\rho_{[a}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}f_{bc]}{}^{d}-f_{e[a}{}^{d}f_{bc]}{}^{e}+\tfrac{1}{3}R^{d|\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{abc}-\psi_{e[ab}C_{c]}{}^{ed}=0,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{C}_{3}{}^{ab|\mu}:=2R^{[a|\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}R^{b]|\mu}-R^{c|\mu}C_{c}{}^{ab}+\rho_{c}{}^{\mu}\varphi^{cab}=0,

(2.12)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{C}_{4}{}_{d}{}^{abc}:=R^{[a|\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}C_{d}{}^{bc]}-C_{d}{}^{e[a}C_{e}{}^{bc]}+\tfrac{1}{3}\rho_{d}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\varphi^{abc}-\varphi^{e[ab}f_{ed}{}^{c]}{}=0,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{C}_{5}{}^{\mu\nu}:=R^{a(\mu}\rho_{a}{}^{\nu)}=0,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{C}_{6}{}_{a}{}^{b|\mu}:=\rho_{a}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}R^{b|\mu}-R^{b|\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\rho_{a}{}^{\mu}-\rho_{c}{}^{\mu}C_{a}{}^{bc}-R^{c|\mu}f_{ca}{}^{b}=0,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{C}_{7}{}_{ab}{}^{cd}:=\rho_{[a}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}C_{b]}{}^{cd}+R^{[c|\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}f_{ab}{}^{d]}+2f_{e[a}{}^{[c}C_{b]}{}^{d]e}-\tfrac{1}{2}f_{ab}{}^{e}C_{e}{}^{cd}-\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{eab}\varphi^{ecd}=0,

(2.16)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{C}_{8}{}^{abcd}:=\tfrac{1}{3}R^{[d|\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\varphi^{abc]}+\tfrac{1}{2}\varphi^{e[ab}C_{e}{}^{cd]}=0,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \mathcal{C}_{9}{}_{abcd}:=\tfrac{1}{3}\rho_{[d}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\psi_{abc]}+\tfrac{1}{2}\psi_{e[ab}f_{cd]}{}^{e}=0.

(2.18)

The latter constraints identify with the component expressions of the defining conditions of a
proto-Lie bialgebroid
on
(E,E^{*})
(compare with (
A.2
)–(
A.6
)). The graded Poisson algebra
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{({\mathscr{E}})}
admits several subalgebras defining in turn various sub-classes of proto-Lie bialgebroids. A convenient way to characterise these Poisson subalgebras is as vanishing ideals of particular Lagrangian submanifolds.
19
19

19

Letting
{\mathcal{N}}
be a (graded) manifold, the
vanishing ideal
of a (graded) submanifold
\mathcal{C}\subset{\mathcal{N}}
is defined as the subalgebra of functions
I_{\mathcal{C}}:=\left\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\left({\mathcal{N}}\right)\,\big|\,f|_{\mathcal{C}}=0\right\}
.
Moreover, a (graded) submanifold
\mathcal{C}
of a (graded) symplectic manifold
({\mathcal{N}},\Omega)
is said to be
Lagrangian
if it is maximally isotropic, i.e.,

1.

The restriction of the symplectic form
\Omega
to
\mathcal{C}
vanishes, i.e.,
\Omega|_{\mathcal{C}}=0
.

2.

The submanifold
\mathcal{C}
has maximal dimension
\dim\mathcal{C}=\frac{1}{2}\dim{\mathcal{N}}
.

For graded Lagrangian submanifolds, it will be assumed that the underlying bosonic manifold of
\mathcal{C}
(whose algebra of functions is coordinatised by coordinates of degree
0
) identifies with the one of
\mathcal{N}
.
Lagrangian submanifolds are coisotropic, so that the corresponding vanishing ideal is closed under the (graded) Poisson bracket associated to
\Omega
and hence is a Poisson subalgebra of the algebra of functions on
{\mathcal{N}}
.
As noted by D. Roytenberg
[
60
]
, both
E[1]
and
E^{*}[1]
are Lagrangian submanifolds of
{\mathscr{E}}
, thus motivating to consider the Poisson subalgebras of functions vanishing on them. We start by defining the Poisson subalgebra
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{({\mathscr{E}})}
as the vanishing ideal of
E[1]
.
In plain words, the subalgebra
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}
is obtained by discarding all functions of the form
\psi_{a_{1}\cdots a_{m}}(x)\xi^{a_{1}}\cdots\xi^{a_{m}}
, for all
m\geq 0
.
The most general Hamiltonian function of
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}
reads as (
2.9
) with
\psi\equiv 0
, where the functions
\left\{\rho,f,R,C,\varphi\right\}
satisfy (
2.10
)–(
2.17
) with
\psi\equiv 0
. In particular, setting
\psi\equiv 0
in equations (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) ensures that the pair
\left\{\rho,f\right\}
defines a genuine Lie algebroid structure on
E
. The resulting equations reproduce the defining conditions of a
Lie-quasi bialgebroid
on
(E,E^{*})
. The pair
\big(({\mathscr{E}},\Omega,{\mathscr{H}}),E[1]\big)
defines a Manin pair,
20
20

20

A
Manin pair
of degree
n
is defined as a dg symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{N},\Omega,{\mathscr{H}})
of degree
n
supplemented with a dg Lagrangian submanifold (also called a
\Lambda
-structure
[
62
]
), i.e., a graded Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{N}
such that
{\mathscr{H}}\in I_{\mathcal{C}}
, with
I_{\mathcal{C}}
the vanishing ideal of
\mathcal{C}
. Manin pairs of degree
0
correspond to a pair formed by a (bosonic) symplectic manifold endowed with a Lagrangian submanifold. Manin pairs of degree
1
are in bijective correspondence with pairs composed of a Poisson manifold together with a coisotropic submanifold while Manin pairs of degree
2
identify with Courant algebroids endowed with a Dirac structure
[
62
]
.
in the terminology of Roytenberg
[
60
]
.

Dually to the previous case, we define the graded Poisson subalgebra
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm quasi-Lie}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{({\mathscr{E}})}
as the vanishing ideal of
E^{*}[1]
, i.e., as the subspace obtained by discarding all functions of the form
\varphi^{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}(x)\zeta_{a_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{a_{n}}
with
n\geq 0
. The most general Hamiltonian function of
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm quasi-Lie}}
reads as (
2.9
) with
\varphi\equiv 0
, where the functions
\left\{\rho,f,R,C,\psi\right\}
satisfy (
2.10
)–(
2.16
) and (
2.18
) with
\varphi\equiv 0
. Dually to the Lie-quasi case, setting
\varphi\equiv 0
in equations (
2.12
)–(
2.13
) ensures that the pair
\left\{R,C\right\}
defines a genuine Lie algebroid structure on
E^{*}
(while the structure defined on
E
by
\left\{\rho,f\right\}
is still “quasi” due to the presence of
\psi
). The resulting equations reproduce the defining conditions of a dual structure on
(E,E^{*})
, dubbed
quasi-Lie bialgebroid
in
[
37
]
. Quasi-Lie bialgebroids are then equivalently characterised as Manin pairs of the form
\big(({\mathscr{E}},\Omega,{\mathscr{H}}),E^{*}[1]\big)
.

We conclude by defining the subspace
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\rm Lie}:=\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}\cap\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm quasi-Lie}}
.
The latter subspace can again be checked to be a graded Poisson subalgebra of
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{({\mathscr{E}})}
obtained by discarding all functions of the form
\psi_{a_{1}\cdots a_{m}}(x)\,\xi^{a_{1}}\cdots\xi^{a_{m}}
or
\varphi^{a_{1}\cdots a_{n}}(x)\zeta_{a_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{a_{n}}
, for all
m,n\geq 0
. In particular, the most general Hamiltonian function of
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\rm Lie}
reads as (
2.9
) with
\varphi\equiv 0
and
\psi\equiv 0
, where the functions
\left\{\rho,f,R,C\right\}
satisfy (
2.10
)–(
2.16
) with
\varphi\equiv 0
and
\psi\equiv 0
. Constraints (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) (resp. (
2.12
)–(
2.13
)) ensure that the pair
\left\{\rho,f\right\}
(resp.
\left\{R,C\right\}
) defines a genuine Lie algebroid structure on
E
(resp.
E^{*}
). These two Lie algebroids are furthermore compatible with each other due to (
2.13
)–(
2.16
) and hence define a
Lie bialgebroid
structure on
(E,E^{*})
(cf. Appendix
A
).
Lie bialgebroids are furthermore equivalently characterised
21
21

21

We refer to Appendix
A
for analogues of these statements within the framework of Courant algebroids.
as Manin triples
22
22

22

A
Manin triple
is a dg symplectic manifold supplemented with two
transverse
dg Lagrangian submanifolds
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\subset\mathcal{N}
such that
{\mathscr{H}}\in I_{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathscr{H}}\in I_{\mathcal{D}}
.
of the form
(({\mathscr{E}},\Omega,{\mathscr{H}}),E[1],E^{*}[1])
.

As usual, gauge transformations for the Hamiltonian function (
2.9
) are generated by functions
23
23

23

Gauge transformations for Lie-quasi (resp. quasi-Lie) bialgebroids are generated by (
2.19
) with
\omega\equiv 0
(resp.
\Lambda\equiv 0
), and with
\Lambda\equiv 0\equiv\omega
for Lie bialgebroids.
of degree
2
in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{({\mathscr{E}})}
reading explicitly:

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}=X^{\mu}p_{\mu}+\lambda^{a}{}_{b}\xi^{b}\zeta_{a}-\tfrac{1}{2}\Lambda^{ab}\zeta_{a}\zeta_{b}-\tfrac{1}{2}\omega_{ab}\xi^{a}\xi^{b},

(2.19)

so that the gauge algebra for proto-Lie bialgebroids is isomorphic to
\Gamma(T\mathscr{M})\oplus\mathsf{End}\big({\Gamma(E)}\big)\oplus\Gamma\big({\wedge}^{2}E\big)\oplus\Gamma\big({\wedge}^{2}E^{*}\big)
as a vector space.
We refer to
[
60
, Section 5]
for a careful treatment of the group structure of these gauge transformations.
The various parameters in (
2.19
) can be interpreted
24
24

24

We refer to Appendix
A
for more details on this interpretation.
in terms of infinitesimal morphisms of proto-Lie bialgebroids as follows:

•

X\in\Gamma(T\mathscr{M})
: diffeomorphism of
\mathscr{M}
,

•

\lambda\in\mathsf{End}\big({\Gamma(E)}\big)
: rotation of the fibers of
(E,E^{*})
,

•

\Lambda\in\Gamma\big({\wedge}^{2}E\big)
twist of Lie-quasi bialgebroids,

•

\omega\in\Gamma\big({\wedge}^{2}E^{*}\big)
twist of quasi-Lie bialgebroids,

where the denomination
twist
refers to the following construction: given a proto-Lie bialgebroid on
(E,E^{*})
defined by the Hamiltonian function
{\mathscr{H}}
, one can define a new proto-Lie bialgebroid structure on
(E,E^{*})
via twisting
25
25

25

The twisting procedure was introduced in
[
16
]
for quasi-Hopf algebras, in
[
35
]
for quasi-Lie bialgebras and in
[
60
]
for quasi-Lie bialgebroids.
by an arbitrary bivector
\Lambda\in\Gamma\big({\wedge}^{2}E\big)
. Explicitly, the twisted proto-Lie bialgebroid
{\mathscr{H}}_{\Lambda}
is obtained by performing a canonical transformation generated by the flow of the Hamiltonian vector field
\mathscr{X}_{\Lambda}:=\big\{{{-}\Lambda},{\cdot}\big\}^{E}_{\Omega}
where
\Lambda:=\frac{1}{2}\Lambda^{ab}\zeta_{a}\zeta_{b}
is a function of degree
2
in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{({\mathscr{E}})}
. Explicitly, a twist by
\Lambda
corresponds to the canonical transformation
26
26

26

Dually, one can consider twisting by a 2-form field
\omega\in\Gamma\big({\wedge}^{2}E^{*}\big)
corresponding to the canonical transformation generated by the flow of the Hamiltonian vector field
\mathscr{X}_{\omega}:=\big\{{-\omega},{\cdot}\big\}
, where
\omega:=\frac{1}{2}\omega_{ab}\,\xi^{a}\xi^{b}
:

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\overset{\omega}{\longrightarrow}x^{\mu},\qquad p_{\mu}\overset{\omega}{\longrightarrow}p_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\omega_{ab}\xi^{a}\xi^{b},\qquad\xi^{a}\overset{\omega}{\longrightarrow}\xi^{a},\qquad\zeta_{a}\overset{\omega}{\longrightarrow}\zeta_{a}-\omega_{ab}\xi^{b}

(2.20)

which amounts to a shift of the components of
{\mathscr{H}}
as in equation (
A.8
).

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\overset{\Lambda}{\longrightarrow}x^{\mu},\qquad p_{\mu}\overset{\Lambda}{\longrightarrow}p_{\mu}-\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\Lambda^{ab}\zeta_{a}\zeta_{b},\qquad\xi^{a}\overset{\Lambda}{\longrightarrow}\xi^{a}-\Lambda^{ab}\zeta_{b},\qquad\zeta_{a}\overset{\Lambda}{\longrightarrow}\zeta_{a},

which amounts to a shift of the components of
{\mathscr{H}}
(cf. equation (
A.7
) in Appendix
A
for explicit expressions). The previous canonical transformation maps Lie-quasi bialgebroids to Lie-quasi bialgebroids but generically fails to preserve quasi-Lie bialgebroids, as is consistent with the fact that
\Lambda\notin\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm quasi-Lie}}
so that
\Gamma\big({\wedge}^{2}E\big)
is not part of the gauge algebra for quasi-Lie bialgebroids.
27
27

27

Dually, the canonical transformation (
2.20
) preserves the space of quasi-Lie bialgebroids, but generically maps Lie-quasi bialgebroids to proto-Lie bialgebroids. This is consistent with the fact that
\omega\notin\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}
so that
\Gamma\big({\wedge}^{2}E^{*}\big)
is not part of the gauge algebra for quasi-Lie bialgebroids.
We refer to Appendix
A
for more details on the twisting procedure.

We sum up the previous discussion in the following proposition, generalising Proposition
2.1
to bialgebroids:

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
E\overset{\pi}{\to}\mathscr{M}
be a vector bundle. The following correspondences hold:

•

Hamiltonians in
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{({\mathscr{E}})}
are in bijective correspondence with proto-Lie bialgebroid structures on
(E,E^{*})
.

•

“
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm Lie-quasi}}
“      Lie-quasi bialgebroid  “ .

•

“
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm quasi-Lie}}
“      quasi-Lie bialgebroid  “ .

•

“
\mathcal{A}^{E}_{\text{\rm Lie}}
“      Lie bialgebroid    “  .

As noted previously, the graded geometric interpretation of algebro-geometric structures is instrumental to the construction of corresponding universal models, formulated in terms of graph complexes. The next section will introduce the relevant graph complexes which will be shown in Section
4
to act on the various (sub)-algebras previously introduced.

3
(Multi)-oriented graph complexes

The aim of this section is to review the definition and main results regarding multi-oriented graph complexes and their cohomology, as introduced and studied in
[
47
,
77
,
78
]
(cf. also
[
48
]
for a review). Graph complexes are most clearly defined as deformation complexes of a suitable morphism of operads
[
49
]
.
28
28

28

Or equivalently, as convolution Lie algebras constructed from suitable graph operads.
We start by introducing the relevant graph operads of multi-directed and multi-oriented graphs from a combinatorial point of view (Section
3.1
) before moving to the definition of the associated graph complexes. We conclude by discussing known results regarding the cohomology of multi-oriented graph complexes (Section
3.2
) by putting the emphasis on some particular classes relevant for our purpose (cf. Section
4
).

3.1
Directed, oriented and sourced graphs

We will denote
\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}
(resp.
\mathsf{dgra}_{N,k}
) the set of multi(di)graphs
29
29

29

Recall that
multi
(
di
)
graphs
are undirected (resp. directed) graphs allowed to contain both loops and multiple edges.
with
N
vertices and
k
directed edges.

The set
\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}
of
multi-directed graphs
with
c
colors is defined as the set of ordered pairs
({\gamma},\mathfrak{c})
where:

•

{\gamma}\in\mathsf{dgra}_{N,k}
is a multidigraph. We will denote
V_{\gamma}
(resp.
E_{\gamma}
) the set of vertices (resp. edges) of
{\gamma}
.

•

\mathfrak{c}
stands for a map
\mathfrak{c}\colon E_{\gamma}\times[c-1]\to\left\{+,-\right\}
where
c\in\mathbb{N}
stands for the total number of
colors
30
30

30

That is, including the underlying (black) arrow of the multidigraph.
and
[c-1]:=\left\{1,2,\dots,c-1\right\}
.

A pictorial representation of a multi-directed graph in
\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}
can be given by decorating each directed edge of the underlying multidigraph in
\mathsf{dgra}_{N,k}
with
c-1
additional arrows of different colors (cf. Figure
1
for an example).

1
2
3
4
i
ii
iii

v

Example of a multi-directed graph in

The direction of the arrow of color

\mathfrak{c}(e,i)=+
and opposite to it if
\mathfrak{c}(e,i)=-
. There is a natural right-action of the permutation group

(resp.

) on elements of
\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}
by permutation of the labeling of vertices (resp. edges).

Operads.
For all
d\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we define the collection
\left\{\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{Gra}_{{d}}(N)\right\}_{N\geq 1}
of

-modules:

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{Gra}_{{d}}(N):=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\big({\mathbb{K}}\left\langle\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}\right\rangle\otimes_{{\mathbb{S}}_{k}}{\text{\rm sgn}}_{k}^{\otimes|d-1|}\big)[k({d}-1)],

(3.1)

where
{\text{\rm sgn}}_{k}
denotes the
1
-dimensional sign representation of
\mathbb{S}_{k}
. The subscript stands for taking coinvariants with respect to the diagonal right action of
{\mathbb{S}}_{k}
and the term between brackets denotes degree suspension. In plain words, this means that edges carry an intrinsic degree
1-{d}
and are bosonic for
d

d
even. The
\mathbb{S}

can further be given the structure of an operad
31
31

Figure 2:
Example of partial composition
\circ_{2}\colon\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}(3)\otimes\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}(2)\to\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}(4)
.

There is a natural sequence of embeddings of operads
32
32

32

Here,
\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\equiv\mathsf{d}_{0}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
(resp.
\mathsf{dGra}_{d}\equiv\mathsf{d}_{1}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
) stands for the operad of one-colored undirected (resp. directed) graphs.

\displaystyle\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\ \overset{{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}}{{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}\ \mathsf{dGra}_{d}\ \overset{{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}}{{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}\ \mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\ \overset{{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}}{{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}\ \mathsf{d}_{3}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\ \overset{{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}}{{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}\ \cdots

(3.2)

given by mapping each graph in
\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{Gra}_{{d}}
to a sum of graphs in
\mathsf{d}_{c+1}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
where the summation runs over all the possible ways to orient the arrow of the additional direction. We call such mapping the
orientation morphism

{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}\colon\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{Gra}_{{d}}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mathsf{d}_{c+1}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
(cf. Figure
3
for an example).

Figure 3:
The orientation morphism
{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}\colon\mathsf{dGra}_{d}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
.

Denoting
\mathsf{Lie}\left\{1-d\right\}
the
(1-d)
-suspended Lie operad,
33
33

33

Recall that representations of
\mathsf{Lie}\left\{1-d\right\}
on a vector space
\mathfrak{g}
are in bijective correspondence with Lie algebra structures on
\mathfrak{g}[1-d]
, hence the graded Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}
has intrinsic degree
1-d
.
there is an operad morphism

\gamma_{0}\colon\ \mathsf{Lie}\left\{1-d\right\}\to\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}

sending the generator

in
\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
.

A multidigraph in
\mathsf{dgra}_{N,k}
will be said
oriented
(or acyclic) if it does not contain cycles.
35
35

35

Recall that a
cycle
(or wheel) is a (non-trivial) directed path from a vertex to itself.
Contrariwise, it will be said non-oriented (or cyclic) if it contains at least one cycle, cf. Figure
4
.

2
1
3

,
2
1
3

Figure 4:
Example of oriented (left) and cyclic (right) graph in
\mathsf{dgra}_{3,3}
.

The subset of oriented multidigraphs will be denoted
\mathsf{ogra}_{N,k}\subset\mathsf{dgra}_{N,k}
. This definition can be extended to multi-directed graphs by defining the subset
36
36

36

By convention, we identify
\mathsf{o}_{0}\mathsf{d}_{0}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}\equiv\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}
,
\mathsf{o}_{0}\mathsf{d}_{1}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}\equiv\mathsf{dgra}_{N,k}
and
\mathsf{o}_{1}\mathsf{d}_{0}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}\equiv\mathsf{ogra}_{N,k}
.

\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}\subseteq\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}
of multi-directed graphs with
c=i+j
directions for which there exists a subset of
i
directions -- black and/or colored -- such that there are no cycles made of the corresponding arrows.
37
37

37

As an example, the graph of Figure
1
belongs to
\mathsf{o}_{2}\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{gra}_{4,5}
as it does not contain cycles for black and yellow arrows (although it does so for red and blue arrows). In the right-hand side of Figure
3
, the first and fourth graphs belong to
\mathsf{o}_{2}\mathsf{d}_{0}\mathsf{gra}_{2,2}
while the second and third graphs belong to
\mathsf{o}_{1}\mathsf{d}_{1}\mathsf{gra}_{2,2}
.
Substituting
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}
in place of
\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{gra}_{N,k}
in (
3.1
) allows to define the collection of
\mathbb{S}_{N}
-modules
\left\{\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}(N)\right\}_{N\geq 1}
for all
i,j,d\geq 0
. It is easy to check that the latter is closed under partial compositions and hence defines a suboperad
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\subseteq\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
of multi-oriented graphs. Note that the graph
1
2
is (trivially) multi-oriented and hence defines a morphism of operads

\gamma_{0}\colon\mathsf{Lie}\left\{1-d\right\}\to\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\qquad\text{for all $i,j,d\geq 0$.}

Oriented graphs belong to the larger subset of multidigraphs possessing a
source
, i.e., a vertex admitting only outgoing arrows. More generally, the suboperad of multi-directed graphs with
c=|k|+j
directions such that
|k|
directions are sourced will be denoted
\mathsf{s}_{k}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\subseteq\mathsf{d}_{c}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
d,j\geq 0
, where negative values of
k
correspond to directions admitting a
sink
, i.e., a vertex with only ingoing arrows. Finally, the suboperad of graphs such that
j
directions admit at least one source and one sink will be denoted
\mathsf{s}_{i,-i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
, for all
i,j,d\geq 0
. It is a well-known result in graph theory that oriented graphs admit at least one source and one sink (see, e.g.,
[
78
]
for a statement) so that we have a sequence of inclusions

\mathsf{o}_{|k|}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\subseteq\mathsf{s}_{|k|,-|k|}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\subseteq\mathsf{s}_{k}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\qquad\text{for all $k\in\mathbb{Z}$ and $d,j\geq 0$}.

Graph complexes.
Given the multi-oriented graph operad
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
, one defines the dg Lie algebra of multi-oriented graphs
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}
as the deformation complex

\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}:=\text{\rm Def}\big(\mathsf{Lie}\left\{1-d\right\}\overset{\gamma_{0}}{\to}\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\big)

of the morphism of operads
\gamma_{0}
.
38
38

38

We refer to references
[
41
,
49
]
for details.
As a graded vector space,
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}
is defined as

\displaystyle\bullet\ \text{${d}$ even:}\ \mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}:=\bigoplus_{N\geq 1}\big(\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}(N)[{d}(1-N)]\big)^{{\mathbb{S}}_{N}},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \text{${d}$ odd:}\ \mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}:=\bigoplus_{N\geq 1}\big(\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}(N)\otimes{\text{\rm sgn}}_{N}[{d}(1-N)]\big)^{{\mathbb{S}}_{N}},

(3.4)

where the terms between brackets denote degree suspension while the superscript stands for taking invariants with respect to the right action of
{\mathbb{S}}_{N}
, with
{\text{\rm sgn}}_{N}
the 1-dimensional signature representation of
{\mathbb{S}}_{N}
. In other words, vertices are bosonic for
d
even and fermionic for
d
odd. According to the degree suspension in (
3.1
) and (
3.3
)–(
3.4
), the degree of an element
39
39

39

Note that the graph degree is insensitive to the number
i
of oriented directions.

{\gamma}\in\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}
with
N
vertices and
k
edges is given by
|{\gamma}|={d}(N-1)+k(1-{d})
.

The graded Lie bracket
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]
of degree 0 on
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}
is defined as usual in terms of the partial composition operations (cf., e.g.,
[
53
, Section 4.2]
for details). The differential
\delta:=\left[\Upsilon_{\mathsf{S}},\cdot\right]
is defined by taking the adjoint action with respect to the Maurer--Cartan element
40
40

40

Note to conclude that the dg Lie algebra
\mathsf{o}_{|k|}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}
of multi-oriented graphs is a sub-dg Lie algebra of the dg Lie algebra of multi-sourced/sinked graphs
\mathsf{s}_{k}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}
defined for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
d,j\geq 0
by
41
41

41

Equivalently, one can define
\mathsf{s}_{k}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}:=\text{\rm Def}\big(\mathsf{Lie}\left\{1-d\right\}\overset{\gamma_{0}}{\to}\mathsf{s}_{k}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}\big)
.
substituting
\mathsf{s}_{k}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}(N)
in place of
\mathsf{o}_{k}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}(N)
in (
3.3
)–(
3.4
).

.

3.2
Cohomology

Having introduced the graph complex of multi-directed graphs as well as its sub-complexes of multi-sourced and multi-oriented graphs, we conclude this review section by collecting some known facts about their respective cohomology.
42
42

42

See
[
47
, Section 5]
and
[
48
, Section 7]
for reviews.
We start by introducing the suboperad
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{cGra}_{d}\subset\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
spanned by
connected
graphs, which in turns yields a sub-dg Lie algebra
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}\subset\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d}
.
43
43

43

Similarly, one can introduce the suboperads of connected multi-directed graphs
\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{cGra}_{d}
and connected multi-sourced graphs
\mathsf{s}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{cGra}_{d}
as well as their corresponding sub-dg Lie algebras
\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}
and
\mathsf{s}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}
.
This is justified by the fact that the cohomology of the graph complex
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
is captured by its connected part,
44
44

44

We refer to
[
71
]
and
[

]
for the cases

respectively and to
[

]
for a general statement.
as follows from

H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fGC}_{d})=\widehat{\mathcal{O}}\big(H^{\bullet}(\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d})[-d]\big)[d],

where
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g})
denotes the completed symmetric algebra associated with the graded vector space
\mathfrak{g}
. As far as cohomology is concerned, one can therefore restrict the analysis to the connected part of the above complexes. Moreover, a further simplification comes from the fact that the cohomology of the various complexes previously introduced can be related to one another. This is embodied by the following important theorem, due to T. Willwacher
45
45

45

See
[

, Appendix K]
for the first item (cf. also

]
),
[

for the second and
[

for the third.
for the case
i=0
and to M. Živković

,

]
for its generalisation to arbitrary
i\geq 0
.

Theorem 3.1
.

For all integers
i,j,d\geq 0
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
:

1.

The inclusion
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j+1}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

2.

There is a quasi-isomorphism
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}\longrightarrow\mathsf{o}_{i+1}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d+1}
.

3.

The inclusion
\mathsf{o}_{|k|}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow\mathsf{s}_{k}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

The quasi-isomorphism of the second item preserves the additional grading provided by the first Betti number.
46
46

46

The
first Betti number
is defined as
b:=k-N+1
for a connected graph with
N
vertices and
k
edges.

A few comments are in order. We start by noting that the sequence of embeddings of operads (
3.2
) induces a sequence
47
47

47

Here, the dg Lie algebra
\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}\equiv\mathsf{d}_{0}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}
(resp.
\mathsf{dfcGC}_{d}\equiv\mathsf{d}_{1}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}
) stands for the usual Kontsevich graph complex of connected undirected (resp. directed) graphs.
of injective quasi-isomorphisms of complexes
48
48

48

See
[
13
,
71
]
for the first arrow and
[
47
]
for a general statement.

\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}\underset{{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}}{\overset{\sim}{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}\mathsf{dfcGC}_{d}\underset{{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}}{\overset{\sim}{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}\mathsf{d}_{2}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}\ \underset{{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}}{\overset{\sim}{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}\ \mathsf{d}_{3}\mathsf{fcGC}_{d}\ \underset{{\mathsf{O}\vec{\mathsf{r}}}}{\overset{\sim}{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}\ \cdots.

Hence, adding extra colored direction does not affect the cohomology. The first item of Theorem
3.1
asserts that this result generalises to multi-oriented graphs where the number
i
of oriented directions is kept fixed.

The situation gets more interesting when orienting extra directions since adding oriented directions
does
change the cohomology. More precisely, the second item of the above theorem relates the cohomology of a given multi-oriented graph complex to the one of a (less oriented) complex in higher dimension. We will comment more on this important result in the next paragraph. Before doing so, let us note that the third item asserts that sourcing directions also affects the cohomology, but in a way that is completely captured by the cohomology of the (smaller) multi-oriented graph complex. Hence, the computation of the cohomology of the multi-sourced/sinked graph complex can always be reduced to computing the cohomology of the multi-oriented graph complex. In the next section, we will therefore express our results in terms of the smaller multi-oriented graph complex when possible.

and

of the (undirected) graph complex in dimensions

Climbing the dimension ladder.

Since the quasi-isomorphism of the second item of Theorem

preserves both the graph degree and the first Betti number, a connected cocycle
{\gamma}\in\mathsf{dgra}_{N,k}
in dimension

and

of

in dimension

is a graph
{\gamma}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{dgra}_{N^{\prime},k^{\prime}}
with
N^{\prime}=N+(d^{\prime}-d)b
and
k^{\prime}=k+(d^{\prime}-d)b
with
b
the first Betti number.
The most striking example of such hierarchy of structures stems from another important theorem of T. Willwacher
[

3.1
, we obtain a sequence

of incarnations of
\mathfrak{grt}_{1}
in arbitrary dimensions
d\geq 2
.

4
Universal models

The aim of the present section is to introduce universal models of multi-oriented graphs (cf. Section
3
) for Lie bialgebroids – and variations thereof – using the graded geometric picture reviewed in Section
2
. We start by providing an abstract characterisation of universal models and emphasise their relevance to address questions related to formality theory and deformation quantization.
We then review universal models for Lie bialgebras (and their “quasi” versions) before moving on to the Lie bialgebroid case. We conclude by discussing the implications of our results regarding the deformation quantization problem for Lie bialgebroids.

4.1
Armchair formality theory

Let
(\mathfrak{g},\delta,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathfrak{g}})
be a dg Lie algebra and denote
(H(\mathfrak{g}),0,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{H(\mathfrak{g})})
the associated cohomology endowed with the canonical dg Lie algebra structure inherited from
\mathfrak{g}
(with trivial differential).
Let furthermore
\Phi\colon(H(\mathfrak{g}),0)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\mathfrak{g},\delta)
be a quasi-isomorphism of complexes. Generically,
\Phi
fails to preserve the additional graded Lie structures – i.e.,
\Phi(\left[x,y\right]_{H(\mathfrak{g})})\neq\left[\Phi(x),\Phi(y)\right]_{\mathfrak{g}}
with
x,y\in H(\mathfrak{g})
– so that
\Phi
is
not
a morphism of dg Lie algebras. According to the
homotopy transfer theorem
,
59
59

59

See, e.g.,
[
41
, Theorem 10.3.1]
for a statement as well as Chapter 10 therein for details and history.
any dg Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is quasi-isomorphic (as a
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-algebra) to its cohomology
H(\mathfrak{g})
endowed with a certain
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-structure
(H(\mathfrak{g}),l)
deforming the canonical dg Lie algebra structure
(H(\mathfrak{g}),0,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{H(\mathfrak{g})})
, i.e.,
l_{1}=0
,
l_{2}=\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{H(\mathfrak{g})}
and the higher order brackets
l_{>2}
are transferred from the dg Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}
. In other words, any quasi-isomorphism of complexes
\Phi\colon(H(\mathfrak{g}),0)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\mathfrak{g},\delta)
can be upgraded to a quasi-isomorphism of
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-algebras
\mathcal{U}\colon(H(\mathfrak{g}),l)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\mathfrak{g},\delta,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathfrak{g}})
with
\mathcal{U}_{1}=\Phi
. If the higher brackets
l_{>2}
vanish, then
(H(\mathfrak{g}),0,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{H(\mathfrak{g})})
and
(\mathfrak{g},\delta,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathfrak{g}})
are quasi-isomorphic as
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-algebras and
\mathfrak{g}
is said to be
formal
. The homotopy transfer theorem thus allows to reduce questions regarding formality (such as existence of formality maps and their classification) to the study of the space of
\mathsf{Lie}_{\infty}
-structures deforming the canonical dg Lie structure
(H(\mathfrak{g}),0,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{H(\mathfrak{g})})
. The relevant deformation theory is controlled by the Chevalley–Eilenberg dg Lie algebra
{\rm\mathsf{CE}}\big(H(\mathfrak{g})\big)
endowed with the Nijenhuis–Richardson bracket
\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{{\mathsf{NR}}}
and the differential
\delta_{\rm\mathsf{S}}:=\big[\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{H(\mathfrak{g})},\cdot\big]_{{\mathsf{NR}}}
. Since we are interested in formality maps given by universal formulae, our aim is to introduce – for each deformation quantization problem at hand – a
universal model
for the deformation theory of
H(\mathfrak{g})
in the guise of a dg Lie algebra of graphs (collectively denoted
\mathsf{GC}
) together with a morphism of dg Lie algebras
\mathsf{GC}\to{\rm\mathsf{CE}}\big(H(\mathfrak{g})\big)
.

Example 4.1

(universal model for polyvector fields)
.

The paradigmatic example of the above construction is due to M. Kontsevich
[
32
]
in the context of the deformation quantization problem for Poisson manifolds.

In this context, the quasi-isomorphism of complexes is provided by the HKR map
\Phi_{\mathsf{HKR}}\colon{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}}\allowbreak\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
between:

•

{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
: the Schouten graded Lie algebra of polyvector fields on the affine space
\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

•

{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
: the Hochschild dg Lie algebra of multidifferential operators on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

According to the previous reasoning, the existence of a formality map
\mathcal{U}\colon{\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}{\mathcal{D}_{\text{\rm poly}}}
can be probed by studying the deformation theory of the Schouten algebra
({\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}},0,\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]_{\mathsf{S}})
, controlled by the Chevalley–Eilenberg dg Lie algebra
{\rm\mathsf{CE}}({\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}})
. In the formulation of his Formality conjecture
[
32
]
, M. Kontsevich introduced a dg Lie algebra of graphs – denoted
\mathsf{fGC}_{2}
– together with a morphism of dg Lie algebras
\mathsf{fGC}_{2}\to{\rm\mathsf{CE}}({\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}})
given by explicit local formulae.
60
60

60

The explicit formulae defining the morphism
\mathsf{fGC}_{2}\to{\rm\mathsf{CE}}({\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}})
take advantage of the graded geometric formulation of Poisson manifolds as dg symplectic manifolds of degree 1, see
[
71
]
for the affine space case (cf. also the earlier work
[
45
]
as well as
[
53
]
for a generalisation to dg symplectic manifolds of arbitrary degree),
[
29
]
for a globalisation to any smooth manifolds and
[
14
]
for a generalisation to the sheaf of polyvector fields on any smooth algebraic variety.
The dg Lie algebra
\mathsf{fGC}_{2}
can therefore be interpreted as a universal model for
{\rm\mathsf{CE}}({\mathcal{T}_{\text{\rm poly}}})
allowing to reduce important questions related to formality theory to the cohomology of
\mathsf{fGC}_{2}
:

•

Existence
: Obstructions to the existence of universal formality maps live in
H^{1}(\mathsf{fGC}_{2})
.

•

Classification
: The space of universal formality maps is classified by
H^{0}(\mathsf{fGC}_{2})
.

This characterisation of the universal solutions to deformation quantization problems via the cohomology of suitable graph complexes can be generalised to other algebro-geometric structures.
In the next sections, we will mimic the Kontsevich construction for Lie bialgebras and Lie bialgebroids by resorting to models of (multi)-oriented graphs.

4.2
Lie bialgebras

We start by reviewing some known results regarding universal models on Lie bialgebras, see, e.g.,
[
50
,
51
,
72
]
. Let us come back to the graded symplectic manifold
T^{*}(\mathfrak{g}[1])\simeq(\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1]
of Section
2.1
endowed with a set of homogeneous local coordinates
\big\{\underset{1}{\xi^{a}},\underset{1}{\zeta_{a}}\big\}
, with
a\in\left\{1,\dots,\dim\mathfrak{g}\right\}
. Using this set of coordinates, one can (locally) endow the graded algebra of functions
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}
with a natural structure of
\mathsf{dGra}_{3}
-algebra, with
\mathsf{dGra}_{3}
the operad of 1-directed graphs in dimension 3. Explicitly, we define a morphism of operads
61
61

61

Here
\mathsf{End}_{V}
stands for the endomorphism operad associated with the (graded) vector space
V
.

{\rm\mathsf{Rep}}^{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathsf{dGra}_{3}\rightarrow\mathsf{End}_{\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}}
via the following sequence of morphisms of graded
62
62

62

Recall that the grading of a graph in
\mathsf{o}_{i}\mathsf{d}_{j}\mathsf{Gra}_{d}
is given by
|\gamma|=k(1-d)
with
k
the number of edges.
vector spaces, for
N\geq 1
:

\displaystyle{\rm\mathsf{Rep}}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{N}\colon\ \mathsf{dGra}_{3}(N)\otimes\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}^{\otimes N}

\displaystyle\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])},

\displaystyle{\rm\mathsf{Rep}}^{\mathfrak{g}}_{N}({\gamma})(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{N})

\displaystyle=\mu_{N}\bigg(\underset{e\in E_{\gamma}}{\prod}\Delta_{e}(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{N})\bigg),

(4.1)

where

•

The
f_{i}
’s are functions on
(\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1]
.

•

The symbol
\mu_{N}
denotes the multiplication map on
N
elements:

\displaystyle\mu_{N}\colon\ \mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])}^{\otimes N}

\displaystyle\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}{((\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}^{*})[1])},

\displaystyle f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{N}

\displaystyle\mapsto f_{1}\cdots f_{N}.

•

The product is performed over the set
E_{\gamma}
of edges of the graph
{\gamma}\in\mathsf{dGra}_{3}(N)
.

•

For each edge
e\in E_{\gamma}
connecting vertices labeled by integers
i
and
j
, the operator
\Delta_{e}
is defined as

\Delta_{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\hbox{\hbox to26.04pt{\vbox to10.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.73637pt\lower-3.80473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.89 0 C 4.89 2.7 2.7 4.89 0 4.89 C -2.7 4.89 -4.89 2.7 -4.89 0 C -4.89 -2.7 -2.7 -4.89 0 -4.89 C 2.7 -4.89 4.89 -2.7 4.89 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.33392pt}{-1.66617pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 30.58 0 C 30.58 2.75 28.35 4.99 25.59 4.99 C 22.84 4.99 20.6 2.75 20.6 0 C 20.6 -2.75 22.84 -4.99 25.59 -4.99 C 28.35 -4.99 30.58 -2.75 30.58 0 Z M 25.59 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{16.8764pt}{-1.18007pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 5.91}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.5359pt}{4.26794pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
